(dmpe)] (dmpe = Me₂PCH₂CH₂PMe₂); [trans-V(η¹-BH₄)₂ (dmpe)₂]; (also B₂H₇⁻, i.e. [BH₃(η¹-BH₄)]⁻)

$\eta^{2}$ : $[\mathrm{Al}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{3}]$ (see p. 230); $[\mathrm{Cu}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})-(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ (Fig. 6.14b); $[\mathrm{Ti}^{\mathrm{III}}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{3}(\mathrm{dme})]$ (dme = 1,2-dimethoxyethane); $[\mathrm{Sc}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{Cp}^{\mathrm{II}})_{2}]$ ( $\mathrm{Cp}^{\mathrm{II}}=\{\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{3}(\mathrm{SiMe}_{3})_{2}\}$ ); $[\mathrm{Y}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})-(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{Cp}^{\mathrm{II}})_{2}(\mathrm{thf})]$

$\eta^{3}$ : $[\mathrm{M}(\eta^{3}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{4}]$ ( $\mathrm{M}=\mathrm{Zr}$ , Hf, Np, Pu; see Fig. 6.14c); $[\mathrm{Ln}(\eta^{3}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{Cp}^{\mathrm{II}})_{2}(\mathrm{thf})]$ ( $\mathrm{Ln}=\mathrm{La}$ , Pr, Nd, Sm); $[\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{IV}}(\eta^{3}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{3}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})]$ $\mu(\eta^{2},\eta^{2})$ : $[\{\mathrm{RuH(tripod)}\}_{2}(\mu:\eta^{2},\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})]^{+}$ (Fig. 6.14d)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mu (\eta^ {3}): [ \mathrm{Co} (\mu : \eta^ {3} - \mathrm{BH} _ {4}) \{\mu - \mathrm{Ph} _ {2} \mathrm{P} (\mathrm{CH} _ {2}) _ {5} \mathrm{PPh} _ {2} \} ] _ {2} (\text {Fig.} \\ 6. 1 4 \mathrm{e}); \quad [ (\text {tmeda}) \mathrm{Li} - \mu (\eta^ {3} - \mathrm{BH} _ {4}) ] _ {2} \quad (\text {tmeda} = \\ \text {tetramethylethylenediamine}) \end{array}
$$

$$
\mu (\eta^ {4}) \colon [ \mathrm{Ce} (\mu : \eta^ {4} - \mathrm{BH} _ {4}) (\eta^ {5} - \mathrm{C} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {3} \mathrm{Bu} _ {2} ^ {t}) ] _ {2} (\text {   Fig.   6.14f   })
$$

Many complexes have more than one coordination mode of $BH_{4}^{-}$ featured in their structure, e.g. $[\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{III}}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})(\eta^{3}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{2}(\mathrm{dmpe})_{2}]$ . Likewise, whereas $[\mathrm{M}(\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{4}]$ are monomeric 12-coordinate complexes for M=Zr, Hf, Np, Pu, they are polymeric for M=Th, Pa, U: the coordination number rises to 14 and each metal centre is coordinated by two $\eta^{3}-BH_{4}^{-}$ and four bridging $\eta^{2}-BH_{4}^{-}$ groups. It is clear that among the factors which determine the mode adopted are the size of the metal atom and the steric requirements of the co-ligands. Many of the complexes are fluxional on an nmr timescale in solution; indeed, this property of fluxionality, which has been increasingly recognized to occur in many inorganic and organometallic systems, was first observed (1955) on the tris-bidentate complex $[\mathrm{Al}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{BH}_{4})_{3}]$ . $^{(38)}$

The $B_{3}H_{8}^{-}$ ion (p. 166) is a triangular cluster of $C_{s}$ (rather than $C_{2v}$ ) symmetry (see Fig. 6.15a); $^{(39)}$ the bridging $H_{\mu}$ atoms are essentially in the $B_{3}$ plane with $H_{t}$ above and below. While it has been conventional to represent the cluster bonding in terms of two BHB and one B–B bond (Fig. 6.15b), recent high-level computations $^{(40)}$ suggest the presence of a 3-centre BBB bond, as depicted approximately in Fig. 6.15c.

The arachno-anion $B_{3}H_{8}^{-}$ is the only binary triboron species that is stable at room temperature and above. It can be viewed as a ligand-stabilized $\{B_{3}H_{7}\}$ group, i.e. $(L.B_{3}H_{7}]$ , in which the ligand is $H^{-}$ (cf. $BH_{4}^{-}$ ). However, the ion is completely fluxional in solution, all three boron atoms (and all eight protons) being equivalent on an nmr timescale. The $B_{3}H_{8}^{-}$ anion has an extensive reaction chemistry both as a reducing agent and as a source of arachno- $B_{4}H_{10}$ (p. 162). Conversely, unsymmetrical (heterolytic) cleavage of $B_{4}H_{10}$ with ligands, L, such as $NH_{3}$ yield $[L_{2}BH_{2}]^{+}[B_{3}H_{8}]^{-}$ .

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/9359ea9e5852f4488f835c290fadf0fedea4919c1d9baffb94de1f87c0e43472.jpg)  
(a)

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/0027cc66f5b899f91eb1aea3e87ca10e37b67feb70e79a9af24640554cf00ec6.jpg)

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/666c3db1d12c5d73ada09999e9f4294083713ff8be907b7fcb105da8497b586a.jpg)  
(c)  
(a) Structure of $B_{3}H_{8}^{-}$ showing $C_{s}$ symmetry; (b) dimensions and representation of the bonding using a direct B–B bond (2013) for the longer (unbridged) B–B distance; (c) most recent (2103) description of the bonding in terms of a 3-centre BBB bond. (See p. 158 for styx formalism.)

The $B_{3}H_{8}^{-}$ ion is also a versatile ligand and forms bidentate and even tridentate complexes with many metal centres. $^{(41)}$ The octahedrally coordinated 18-electron manganese(I) complex $[\mathrm{Mn}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{B}_{3}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{CO})_{4}]$ is a particularly instructive example. As can be seen from Fig. 6.16a it has a cluster structure that is clearly related to that of $B_{4}H_{10}$ (13). When heated to $180^{\circ}C$ or irradiated with ultraviolet light the complex loses one of the four CO ligands and this enables a further B–H group to coordinate to give the trihapto complex fac- $[\mathrm{Mn}(\eta^{3}-\mathrm{B}_{3}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{CO})_{3}]$ (Fig. 6.16b). Treatment of this product with an excess of CO under moderate pressure regenerates the original dihapto species by a simple ligand replacement reaction. $^{(42)}$

## 6.4.5 Intermediate-sized Boranes and their Anions ( $B_{5}-B_{9}$ )

Pentaborane(9), $nido-B_{5}H_{9}$ , is by far the most studied borane in this group. It can be prepared by passing a 1:5 mixture of $\mathrm{B}_{2}\mathrm{H}_{6}$ and $\mathrm{H}_{2}$ at subatmospheric pressure through a furnace at $250^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ with a residence time of 3 s (or at $225^{\circ}$ with a 15 s residence time); there is a $70\%$ yield and $30\%$ conversion. Alternatively $\mathrm{B}_{2}\mathrm{H}_{6}$ can be pyrolysed for 2.5 days in a static hot/cold reactor at $180^{\circ}/-80^{\circ}$ . $\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{9}$ is a colourless, volatile liquid, bp $60.0^{\circ}$ ; it is thermally stable but chemically very reactive and spontaneously flammable in air. Its structure is essentially a square-based pyramid of B atoms each of which carries a terminal H atom and there are 4 bridging H atoms around the base (structure 9, p. 154). The slant edge of the pyramid, $\mathrm{B}(1)-\mathrm{B}(2)$ , is $168\mathrm{pm}$ and the basal interboron distances, $\mathrm{B}(2)-\mathrm{B}(3)$ etc, are $178\mathrm{pm}$ ; other key dimensions are $\mathrm{B}-\mathrm{H}_{t} 122\mathrm{pm}$ , $\mathrm{B}-\mathrm{H}_{\mu} 135\mathrm{pm}$ and $\mathrm{B}-\mathrm{H}_{\mu}-\mathrm{B} 83^{\circ}$ . Calculations suggest that $\mathrm{B}(1)$ has a slightly higher electron density than the basal borons and that $\mathrm{H}_{\mu}$ is slightly more positive than $\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{t}}$ . Apex-substituted derivatives $1-\mathrm{XB}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8}$ can readily be prepared by electrophilic substitution (e.g. halogenation or Friedel-Crafts alkylation with RX or alkenes), whereas base-substituted derivatives $2-XB_{5}H_{8}$ result when nucleophilic reaction is induced by amines or ethers, or when $1-XB_{5}H_{8}$ is isomerized in the presence of a Lewis base such as hexamethylenetetramine or an ether:

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/77f3ff4a2de1cdc6b85df36f962fac535ac7c4bea2068d691320c3f0c40768b0.jpg)  
Figure 6.16 Ligand replacement reaction of $[\mathrm{Mn}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{B}_{3}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{CO})_{4}]$ (see text).

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{B} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {9} & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{I} _ {2} / \mathrm{AlX} _ {3}} 1 - \mathrm{IB} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {8} \xrightarrow [ (\mathrm{CH} _ {2}) _ {6} \mathrm{N} _ {4} ]{\text {base}} 2 - \mathrm{IB} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {8} \\ & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{SbF} _ {3}} 2 - \mathrm{FB} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {8} \end{array}
$$

Further derivatives can be obtained by metathesis, e.g.

$$
\begin{array}{r l} 2 - \mathrm{ClB} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {8} + \mathrm{NaMn(CO)} _ {5} & \longrightarrow \\ 2 - \{(\mathrm{CO}) _ {5} \mathrm{Mn} \} \mathrm{B} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {8} & (\text { also   Re }) \end{array}
$$

$B_{5}H_{9}$ reacts with Lewis bases (electron-pair donors) to form adducts, some of which have now been recognized as belonging to the new series of hypho-borane derivatives $B_{n}H_{n+8}$ (p. 152). Thus $PMe_{3}$ gives the adduct $[B_{5}H_{9}(PMe_{3})_{2}]$ which is formally analogous to $[B_{5}H_{11}]^{2-}$ and the (unknown) borane $B_{5}H_{13}$ . $[B_{5}H_{9}(PMe_{3})_{2}]$ has a very open structure in the form of a shallow pyramid with the ligands attached at positions 1 and 2 and with major rearrangement of the H atoms (Fig. 6.17a). Chelating phosphine ligands such as $(Ph_{2}P)_{2}CH_{2}$ and $(\mathrm{Ph}_{2}\mathrm{PCH}_{2})_{2}$ have similar structures but $[\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{9}(\mathrm{Me}_{2}\mathrm{NCH}_{2}\mathrm{CH}_{2}\mathrm{NMe}_{2})]$ undergoes a much more severe distortion in which the ligand chelates a single boron atom, B(2), which is joined to the rest of the molecule by a single bond to the apex B(1) (Fig. 6.17b). $^{(43)}$ With $NH_{3}$ as ligand (at $-78^{\circ}$ ) complete excision of one B atom occurs by “unsymmetrical cleavage” to give $[(\mathrm{NH}_{3})_{2}\mathrm{BH}_{2}]^{+}[\mathrm{B}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{7}]^{-}$

$B_{5}H_{9}$ also acts as a weak Brønsted acid and, from proton competition reactions with other boranes and borane anions, it has been established that acidity increases with increasing size of the borane cluster and that arachno-boranes are more acidic than nido-boranes:

$$
n i d o: \mathrm{B} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {9} <   \mathrm{B} _ {6} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0} <   \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} <   \mathrm{B} _ {1 6} \mathrm{H} _ {2 0} <   \mathrm{B} _ {1 8} \mathrm{H} _ {2 2}
$$

arachno: $\mathrm{B}_4\mathrm{H}_{10} <   \mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_{11} <   \mathrm{B}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}$ and

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {4} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0} > \mathrm{B} _ {6} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0}
$$

Accordingly, $B_{5}H_{9}$ can be deprotonated at low temperatures by loss of $H_{\mu}$ to give $B_{5}H_{8}^{-}$ providing a sufficiently strong base such as a lithium alkyl or alkali metal hydride is used. Bridge-substituted derivatives of $B_{5}H_{9}$ can then be obtained by reacting $MB_{5}H_{8}$ with chloro compounds such as $R_{2}PCl$ , $Me_{3}SiCl$ , $Me_{3}GeCl$ ,

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/a67b195d32dd6e88df8d274b18d75dc3f5a2a60d687854a8820ea5c025cd4e6d.jpg)  
(a) $[B_{5}H_{9}(PMe_{3})_{2}]$  
(b) $[B_{5}H_{9}(Me_{2}NCH_{2}CH_{2}NMe_{2})]$  
Figure 6.17 Structure of hypho-borane derivatives: (a) $[B_{5}H_{9}(PMe_{3})_{2}]$ — the distances B(1)-B(2) and B(2)-B(3) are as in $B_{5}H_{9}$ (p. 170) but B(3)···B(4) is 295 pm (cf. B···B 297 pm in $B_{5}H_{11}$ , structure 14, p. 154), and (b) $[B_{5}H_{9}(Me_{2}NCH_{2}CH_{2}NMe_{2})]$ — the distances B(2)···B(3) and B(2)···B(5) are 273 and 272 pm respectively.

or even $Me_{2}BCl$ to give compounds in which the 3-centre $B-H_{\mu}-B$ bond has been replaced by a 3-centre bond between the 2 B atoms and P, Si, Ge or B respectively. Many metal-halide coordination complexes react similarly, and the products can be considered as adducts in which the $B_{5}H_{8}^{-}$ anion is acting formally as a 2-electron ligand via a 3-centre B-M-B bond. $^{(44,45)}$ Thus $[\mathrm{Cu}^{\mathrm{I}}(\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ (Fig. 6.18a) is readily formed by the low-temperature reaction of $KB_{5}H_{8}$ with $[\mathrm{CuCl}(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{3}]$ and analogous 16-electron complexes have been prepared for many of the later transition elements, e.g. $[\mathrm{Cd}(\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})\mathrm{Cl}(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})]$ , $[\mathrm{Ag}(\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ and $[\mathrm{M}^{\mathrm{II}}(\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})\mathrm{XL}_{2}]$ , where $M^{II}=Ni$ , Pd, Pt; X=Cl, Br, I; $L_{2}=a$ diphosphine or related ligand. By contrast, $[\mathrm{Ir}^{\mathrm{I}}(\mathrm{CO})\mathrm{Cl}(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ reacts by oxidative insertion of Ir and consequent cluster expansion to give $[(\mathrm{IrB}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{CO})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ which, though superficially of similar formula, has the structure of an irida-nido-hexaborane (Fig. 6.18b). $^{(46)}$ In this, the $\{\mathrm{Ir}(\mathrm{CO})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}\}$ moiety replaces a basal $BH_{1}H_{\mu}$ unit in $B_{6}H_{10}$ (structure 10, p. 154).

Cluster-expansion and cluster-degradation reactions are a feature of many polyhedral borane species. Examples of cluster-expansion are: $^{(11,47)}$

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm {LiB_ {5} H_ {8}} + \frac {1}{2} \mathrm {B_ {2} H_ {6}} & \xrightarrow [ (\text {fast}) ]{\mathrm {Et_ {2} O / - 78^ {\circ}}} \mathrm {LiB_ {6} H_ {11}} \\ & \xrightarrow [ ]{\mathrm{HCl}} \mathrm {B_ {6} H_ {12}} \\ \mathrm {LiB_ {5} H_ {8}} + \mathrm {B_ {2} H_ {6}} & \xrightarrow [ (\text {slow}) ]{\mathrm {Me_ {2} O / - 78^ {\circ}}} \{\mathrm {LiB_ {7} H_ {14}} \} \\ & \longrightarrow \mathrm {B_ {6} H_ {10}} + \mathrm {LiBH_ {4}} \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{r l} 1. 8 \mathrm{B} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {9} + \mathrm{MH} & \xrightarrow [ 1 5 \mathrm{h} ]{\text {thf / r.t.}} \mathrm{MB} _ {9} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + \mathrm{H} _ {2} \text {plus} \\ & \text {minor products (M = Na,K)} \end{array}
$$

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/d9a3de66a5c33ee2397b96be12ca1a8ede9d1e5451e177fd9d2faa0fee66d599.jpg)

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/521df0f42d11dd7339e88d0d06b35a449a2b753589f991e07bcb6d85b0a90a36.jpg)  
Figure 6.18 (a) Structure of $[\mathrm{Cu}(\mathrm{B}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ , showing $\eta^{2}$ -bonding of $B_{5}H_{8}^{-}$ (phenyl groups omitted for clarity); (b) Structure of $[(\mathrm{IrB}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{8})(\mathrm{CO})(\mathrm{PPh}_{3})_{2}]$ showing the structure about the iridium atom and the relationship of the metallaborane cluster to that of $nido-B_{6}H_{10}$ .

$$
\mathrm{KB} _ {9} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 0. 4 \mathrm{B} _ {5} \mathrm{H} _ {9} \xrightarrow [ 2 0 \mathrm{h} ]{\text {glyme/85°}} \mathrm{KB} _ {1 1} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 1. 8 \mathrm{H} _ {2}
$$

Cluster degradation has already been mentioned in connection with the unsymmetrical cleavage reaction (p. 165) and other examples are:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & [ \mathrm {NM e _ {4}} ] \mathrm {B _ {5} H _ {8}} + 6 \mathrm{Pr} ^ {i} \mathrm{OH} \longrightarrow [ \mathrm {NM e _ {4}} ] \mathrm {B _ {3} H _ {8}} \\ & \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad + 2 \mathrm{B} (\mathrm{OPr} ^ {i}) _ {3} + 3 \mathrm {H _ {2}} \\ & \text { and } \mathrm {B _ {5} H _ {9}} \xrightarrow {\text { tmed }} \mathrm {B _ {5} H _ {9} (tmed)} \xrightarrow {\text { MeOH }} \mathrm {B _ {4} H _ {8} (tmed)} \\ & \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad + \mathrm{B(OMe)} _ {3} + 2 \mathrm {H _ {2}} \end{array}
$$

(where $\mathbf{Pr}^i$ is $\mathbf{Me}_2\mathbf{CH}-$ and tmed is $\mathbf{Me}_2\mathbf{NCH}_2-$ $\mathbf{CH}_2\mathbf{NMe}_2$ ).

Replacement of a $\{BH\}$ unit in $B_{5}H_{9}$ by an “isoelectronic” organometallic group such as $\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}$ or $\{\mathrm{Co}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})\}$ can also occur, and this illustrates the close interrelation between metalloboranes, metal–metal cluster compounds, and organometallic complexes in general (see Panel).

The structures of several other nido- and arachno- $\mathbf{B}_5 - \mathbf{B}_9$ boranes are given on page 154 but a detailed discussion of their chemistry is beyond the scope of this treatment. Further information is in refs. 9, 11, 27, 51 and 52.

## 6.4.6 Chemistry of nido-decaborane, $B_{10}H_{14}$

Decaborane is the most studied of all the polyhedral boranes and at one time (mid-1950s) was manufactured on a multitonne scale in the USA as a potential high-energy fuel. It is now obtainable in research quantities by the pyrolysis of $B_{2}H_{6}$ at 100–200°C in the presence of catalytic amounts of Lewis bases such as $Me_{2}O$ . $B_{10}H_{14}$ is a colourless, volatile, crystalline solid (see Table 6.2, p. 163) which

## Metalloboranes, Metal Clusters and Organometallic Complexes

Copyrolysis of $B_{5}H_{9}$ and $[\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{5}]$ in a hot/cold reactor at $220^{\circ}/20^{\circ}$ for 3 days gives an orange liquid (mp $5^{\circ}C$ ) of formula $[1-\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}\mathrm{B}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{8}]$ having the structure shown in (a). $^{(48)}$ The isoelectronic complex $[1-\{\mathrm{Co}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})\}\mathrm{B}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{8}]$ (structure b) can be obtained as yellow crystals by pyrolysis at $200^{\circ}$ of the corresponding basal derivative $[2-\{\mathrm{Co}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})\}\mathrm{B}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{8}]$ (structure (c)) which is obtained as red crystals from the reaction of $NaB_{5}H_{8}$ and $CoCl_{2}$ with $NaC_{5}H_{5}$ in thf at $-20^{\circ}$ . $^{(49)}$ The course of these reactions is obscure and other products are also obtained.

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/9a525b55b81832f2dff0effba757ccf9289eef1c44cde7e2b6ca56fdbce0be7c.jpg)

As $\{\mathrm{BH}_{2}\}$ is isoelectronic with $\{\mathrm{CH}\}$ . these metalloborane clusters are isoelectronic with the cyclobutadiene adduct $[\mathrm{Fe}(\eta^{4}-\mathrm{C}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{4})(\mathrm{CO})_{3}]$ , see (d), (e) and (f). Likewise $\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}$ or $\{\mathrm{Co}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})\}$ can replace a $\{\mathrm{BH}\}$ group in $\mathbf{B}_{5}\mathbf{H}_{9}$ and two descriptions of the bonding are given in (g) and (h): the Fe atom supplies 2 electrons and 3 atomic orbitals to the cluster (as does BH), thereby enabling it to form 2 Fe-B $\sigma$ bonds and to accept a pair of electrons from adjacent B atoms to form a 3-centre BMB bond. In this description the Fe atom is formally octahedral $\mathrm{Fe}^{\mathrm{II}}$ ( $d^{6}$ ). Alternatively, diagram (h) emphasizes the relation between $[1-\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}\mathrm{B}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{8}]$ and $[\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}(\eta^{4}-\mathrm{C}_{4}\mathrm{H}_{4})]$ or $[\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{5}]$ : the Fe atom accepts 2 pairs of electrons to form two 3-centre BMB bonds and is formally $\mathrm{Fe}^{0}$ with a trigonal bipyramidal arrangement of bonds.

It is possible to replace more than one $\{BH\}$ group in $B_{5}H_{9}$ by a metal centre, e.g. in the dimetalla species $[1,2-\{Fe(CO)_{3}\}_{2}B_{3}H_{7}]^{(50)}$ it is also notable that the iron carbonyl cluster compound $\{Fe_{5}(CO)_{15}C\}$ (p. 1108) features the same square-pyramidal cluster in which 5 $\{Fe(CO)_{3}\}$ groups have replaced the five $\{BH\}$ groups in $B_{5}H_{9}$ , and the C atom (in the centre of the base) replaces the 4 bridging H atoms by supplying the 4 electrons required to complete the bonding.

Many other equivalent groups can be envisaged and the formalism permits a unified approach to possible synthetic routes and to probable structures of a wide variety of compounds. $^{[17,51-53]}$

$^{48}$ N. N. GREENWOOD, C. G. SAVORY, R. N. GRIMES, L. G. SNEDDON, A. DAVISON and S. S. WREFORD, J. Chem. Soc., Chem. Commun., 718 (1974).

$^{49}$ V. R. MILLER and R. N. GRIMES, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 95, 5078–80 (1973).

$^{50}$ K. J. HALLER, E. L. ANDERSEN and T. P. FEHLNER, Inorg. Chem. 20, 309–13 (1981).

$^{51}$ N. N. GREENWOOD and J. D. KENNEDY, Chap. 2 in R. N. GRIMES (ed.), Metal Interactions with Boron Clusters, Plenum, New York, 1982, pp. 43–118.

$^{52}$ J. D. KENNEDY, Prog. Inorg. Chem. 32, 519–679 (1984); 34, 211–434 (1986).

$^{53}$ T. P. FEHLNER (ed.), Inorganometallic Chemistry, Plenum, New York, 1992, 401 pp.

is insoluble in $H_{2}O$ but readily soluble in a wide range of organic solvents. Its structure (36) can be regarded as derived from the 11 B atom cluster $B_{11}H_{11}^{2-}$ (p. 153) by replacing the unique BH group with 2 electrons and appropriate addition of $4H_{\mu}$ . MO-calculations give the sequence of electron charge densities at the various B atoms as 2, 4 > 1, 3 > 5, 7, 8, 10 > 6, 9 though the total range of deviation from charge neutrality is less than ±0.1 electron per B atom. The chemistry of $B_{10}H_{14}$ can be conveniently discussed under the headings (a) proton abstraction, (b) electron addition, (c) adduct formation, (d) cluster rearrangements, cluster expansions, and cluster degradation reactions, and (e) metalloborane and other heteroborane compounds.

$B_{10}H_{14}$ can be titrated in aqueous/alcoholic media as a monobasic acid, $pK_{a}$ 2.70:

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + \mathrm{OH} ^ {-} \rightleftharpoons \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} ^ {-} + \mathrm{H} _ {2} \mathrm{O}
$$

Proton abstraction can also be effected by other strong bases such as $H^{-}$ , $OMe^{-}$ , $NH_{2}^{-}$ , etc. The $B_{10}H_{13}^{-}$ ion is formed by loss of a bridge proton, as expected, and this results in a considerable shortening of the B(5)–B(6) distance from 179 pm in $B_{10}H_{14}$ to 165 pm in $B_{10}H_{13}^{-}$ (structures 36, 37). Under more forcing conditions with NaH a second $H_{\mu}$ can be removed to give $Na_{2}B_{10}H_{12}$ ; the probable structure of $B_{10}H_{12}^{2-}$ is (38) and the anion acts as a formal bidentate (tetrahapto) ligand to many metals (p. 177).

Electron addition to $B_{10}H_{14}$ can be achieved by direct reaction with alkali metals in ethers, benzene or liquid $NH_{3}$ :

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{Na} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Na} _ {2} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4}
$$

A more convenient preparation of the $B_{10}H_{14}^{2-}$ anion uses the reaction of aqueous $BH_{4}^{-}$ in alkaline solution:

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} \xrightarrow {+ \mathrm{BH} _ {4} ^ {-} - \{\mathrm{BH} _ {3} \}} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 5} ^ {-} \underset {+ \mathrm{H} ^ {+}} {\overset {- \mathrm{H} ^ {+}} {\rightleftharpoons}} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} ^ {2 -}
$$

Structure (39) conforms to the predicted (2632) topology (p. 158) and shows that the 2 added electrons have relieved the electron deficiency to the extent that the $2\ B-H_{\mu}-B$ groups have been converted to B–H $_{t}$ with the consequent appearance of 2BH $_{2}$ groups in the structure. Calculations show that this conversion of a nido- to an arachno-cluster reverses the sequence of electron charge density at the 2, 4 and 6, 9 positions so that for B $_{10}$ H $_{14}$ $^{2-}$ the sequence is 6, 9 > 1, 3 > 5, 7, 8, 10 > 2, 4; this is paralleled by changes in the chemistry. B $_{10}$ H $_{14}$ $^{2-}$ can formally be regarded as B $_{10}$ H $_{12}$ L $_{2}$ for the special case of L = H $^{-}$ . Compounds of intermediate stoichiometry B $_{10}$ H $_{13}$ L $^{-}$ are formed when B $_{10}$ H $_{14}$ is deprotonated in the presence of the ligand L:

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/8a1f6dff0f04ca82442ea8c389b1657623d952e65766f89b158bda5131242307.jpg)

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O} / \mathrm{NaH}} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} ^ {-} \xrightarrow {\mathrm{Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O} / \mathrm{L}} [ \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} \mathrm{L} ] ^ {-} \\ & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{NMe} _ {4} ^ {+}} [ \mathrm{NMe} _ {4} ] [ \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} \mathrm{L} ] \end{array}
$$

The adducts $B_{10}H_{12}L_{2}$ (structure 40) can be prepared by direct reaction of $B_{10}H_{14}$ with L or by ligand replacement reactions:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm {B_ {10} H_ {14} + 2MeCN\longrightarrow B_ {10} H_ {12} (MeCN) _ {2} + H_ {2}}} \\ & {\mathrm {B_ {10} H_ {12} L_ {2} + 2L^ {\prime} \longrightarrow B_ {10} H_ {12} L_ {2} ^ {\prime} + 2L}} \end{array}
$$

Ligands L, L' can be drawn from virtually the full range of inorganic and organic neutral and anionic ligands and, indeed, the reaction severely limits the range of donor solvents in which $B_{10}H_{14}$ can be dissolved. The approximate sequence of stability is:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{SR} _ {2} <   \mathrm{RCN} <   \mathrm{AsR} _ {3} <   \mathrm{RCONMe} _ {2} <   \mathrm{P(OR)} _ {3} \\ & <   \mathrm{py} \approx \mathrm{NEt} _ {3} \approx \mathrm{PPh} _ {3} \end{array}
$$

The stability of the phosphine adducts is notable as is the fact that thioethers readily form such adducts whereas ethers do not. Bis-ligand adducts of moderate stability play an important role in activating decaborane for several types of reaction to be considered in more detail in subsequent paragraphs, e.g.:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \text {Substitution:} & \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2} (\mathrm{SR} _ {2}) _ {2} + \mathrm{HX} \xrightarrow {\mathrm{C} _ {6} \mathrm{H} _ {6} / 2 0 ^ {\circ}} \\ & 6 - (5 -) \mathrm{XB} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} (\mathrm{X} = \mathrm{F}, \mathrm{Cl}, \mathrm{Br}, \mathrm{I}) \end{array}
$$

Cluster rearrangement: arachno- $\mathrm{B}_{10}\mathrm{H}_{12}(\mathrm{NEt}_3)_2$

$$
\longrightarrow [ \mathrm{NEt} _ {3} \mathrm{H} ] ^ {+} _ {2} [ c l o s o - \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0} ] ^ {2 -}
$$

Cluster addition: arachno- $B_{10}H_{12}(SR_{2})_{2}$ +

$$
\begin{array}{r l} 2 \mathrm{RC} & \equiv \mathrm{CR} \longrightarrow c l o s o - \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0} (\mathrm{CR}) _ {2} \\ & + 2 \mathrm{SR} _ {2} + \mathrm{H} _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Cluster degradation: $B_{10}H_{12}L_{2} + 3ROH \longrightarrow$

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {9} \mathrm{H} _ {1 3} \mathrm{L} + \mathrm{B} (\mathrm{OR}) _ {3} + \mathrm{L} + \mathrm{H} _ {2}
$$

In this last reaction it is the coordinated B atom at position 9 that is solvolytically cleaved from the cluster.

Electrophilic substitution of $B_{10}H_{14}$ follows the sequence of electron densities in the ground-state molecule. Thus halogenation in the presence of $AlCl_{3}$ leads to 1- and 2-monosubstituted derivatives and to 2,4-disubstitution. Similarly, Friedel–Crafts alkylations with RX/ $AlCl_{3}$ (or $FeCl_{3}$ ) yield mixtures such as 2- $MeB_{10}H_{13}$ , 2,4- and 1,2- $Me_{2}B_{10}H_{12}$ , 1,2,3- and 1,2,4- $Me_{3}B_{10}H_{11}$ , and 1,2,3,4- $Me_{4}B_{10}H_{10}$ . By contrast, nucleophilic substitution (like the adduct formation with Lewis bases) occurs preferentially at the 6 (9) position; e.g., LiMe produces 6- $MeB_{10}H_{13}$ as the main product with smaller amounts of 5- $MeB_{10}H_{13}$ , 6,5(8)- $Me_{2}B_{10}H_{12}$ and 6,9- $Me_{2}B_{10}H_{12}$ .

$B_{10}H_{14}$ undergoes numerous cluster-addition reactions in which B or other atoms become incorporated in an expanded cluster. Thus in a reaction which differs from that on p. 175 $BH_{4}^{-}$ adds to $B_{10}H_{14}$ with elimination of $H_{2}$ to form initially the $nido-B_{11}H_{14}^{-}$ anion (structure 41, p. 178) and then the $closo-B_{12}H_{12}^{2-}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + \mathrm{LiBH} _ {4} \xrightarrow {\text {monoglyme/90°}} \mathrm{LiB} _ {1 1} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{H} _ {2} \\ & \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{LiBH} _ {4} \xrightarrow {} \mathrm{Li} _ {2} \mathrm{B} _ {1 2} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2} + 5 \mathrm{H} _ {2} \end{array}
$$

A more convenient high-yield synthesis of $B_{12}H_{12}^{2-}$ is by the direct reaction of amine-boranes with $B_{10}H_{14}$ in the absence of solvents:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{Et} _ {3} \mathrm{NBH} _ {3} & \xrightarrow {9 0 - 1 0 0 ^ {\circ}} \\ & [ \mathrm{NEt} _ {3} \mathrm{H} ] _ {2} ^ {+} [ \mathrm{B} _ {1 2} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2} ] ^ {2 -} + 3 \mathrm{H} _ {2} \end{array}
$$

Heteroatom cluster addition reactions are exemplified by the following:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + \mathrm{Me} _ {3} \mathrm{NAlH} _ {3} & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O}} \\ & [ \mathrm{NMe} _ {3} \mathrm{H} ] ^ {+} [ \mathrm{AlB} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} ] ^ {-} + \mathrm{H} _ {2} \\ \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{TlMe} _ {3} & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O}} \\ & [ \mathrm{TlMe} _ {2} ] ^ {+} [ \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2} \mathrm{TlMe} _ {2} ] ^ {-} \end{array}
$$

## The Concept of Boranes as Ligands

Boranes are usually regarded as being electron-deficient, in the sense that they have an insufficient number of electrons to form classical 2-centre 2-electron bonds between each contiguous pair of atoms. However, in the mid-1960s several groups began to realize that, far from being deficient in electrons, many boranes and their anions could act as very effective polyhapto ligands: that is, they could form donor–acceptor complexes (coordination compounds, Chap. 19) in which the borane cluster itself was acting as the electron donor or ligand. The application of this astonishing idea has extended enormously the range of boron hydride compounds which can be made. $^{[54]}$ Many aspects have already been alluded to in the preceding pages and these are briefly summarized in this Panel.

Boranes can act as ligands either by forming 3-centre. 2-electron B-H-M bonds (analogous to BHB bonds) or by forming direct $\mathbf{B}_n\mathbf{M}$ bonds ( $n = 1-6$ , analogous to B-B, BBB etc bonds). All hapticities from $\eta^1 - \eta^6$ and occasionally beyond are known. The various coordination modes of $\mathrm{BH}_4^-$ and $\mathrm{B}_3\mathrm{H}_8^-$ were discussed on pp. 168-71: these involve the conversion of $\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{H}_t$ bonds to $\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{H} \to \mathbf{M}$ bonds. Likewise, the use of $\mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_8^-$ as an $\eta^2$ -ligand was described on pp. 172-3. this involves the notional donation to a metal centre of the electron pair in a B-B bond, thus forming a BMB 3-centre bond. $\mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_8^-$ can also act as a notional $\eta^1$ -donor by replacement of a terminal H atom in $\mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_9$ with a metal centre: e.g. direct reaction of $\mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_8\mathrm{Cl}$ or $\mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_8\mathrm{Br}$ with $\mathrm{NaM(CO)_5}$ to give $[\mathrm{M}(\eta^1 - 2 - \mathrm{B}_5\mathrm{H}_8)(\mathrm{CO})_5]$ ( $\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{Mn}$ , Re).

It is clear that some boranes are amphoteric Lewis acid/bases — that is they can act either as electron-pair donors as above or as electron-pair acceptors (e.g. in L.BH $_3$ and L.B $_3$ H $_7$ ). It follows that a borane donor could conceivably ligate to a borane acceptor to form a borane–borane complex, i.e. a larger borane, e.g. BH $_4^-$ + {BH $_3$ } \longrightarrow B $_2$ H $_7^-$ (p. 154). In this sense B $_2$ H $_6$ itself could be regarded either as a coordination complex of BH $_4^-$ with the notional cation {BH $_2^+$ }, or as the mutual coordination of two monodentate {BH $_3$ } units. Replacement of these donors with stronger ligands such as NH $_3$ or NMe $_3$ would then result in either unsymmetrical or symmetrical cleavage of B $_2$ H $_6$ as discussed on p. 165. Likewise, B $_4$ H $_{10}$ could be regarded either as a complex between $\eta^2$ -B $_3$ H $_8^-$ and {BH $_2^+$ } or as a mutual coordination between {B $_3$ H $_7$ } and {BH $_3$ }; reaction with stronger ligands, L, would then yield either [L $_2$ BH $_2]^+[\text{B}_3\text{H}_8]^-$ or L.B $_3$ H $_7$ and L.BH $_3$ by ligand displacement reactions (pp. 169–70).

The neutral nido-borane $B_{6}H_{10}$ (structure 10) has a basal B-B bond (see p. 159) and this enables it to act as a ligand by displacing ethene from Zeise's salt (p. 930).

$$
\mathrm{K} \left[ \mathrm{Pt} \left(\eta^ {2} - \mathrm{C} _ {2} \mathrm{H} _ {4}\right) \mathrm{Cl} _ {3} \right] + 2 \mathrm{B} _ {6} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0} \longrightarrow t r a n s - \left[ \mathrm{Pt} \left(\eta^ {2} - \mathrm{B} _ {6} \mathrm{H} _ {1 0}\right) _ {2} \mathrm{Cl} _ {2} \right] + \mathrm{C} _ {2} \mathrm{H} _ {4} + \mathrm{KCl}
$$

Similarly, reaction of $B_{6}H_{10}$ with $\mathrm{Fe}_{2}(\mathrm{CO})_{9}$ (p. 1104) at room temperature results in the smooth elimination of $\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{5}$ to form $[\mathrm{Fe}(\eta^{2}-\mathrm{B}_{6}\mathrm{H}_{10})(\mathrm{CO})_{4}]$ as a stable, volatile yellow solid. Use of these electron-donor properties of $B_{6}H_{10}$ towards reactive (vacant orbital) borane radicals resulted in the preparation of several new conjuncto-boranes, e.g. $B_{13}H_{19}$ , $B_{14}H_{22}$ and $B_{15}H_{23}$ (p. 162).

Another important concept is the notion of stabilization by means of coordination. A classic example is the stabilization of the fugitive species cyclobutadiene, $\{C_{4}H_{4}\}$ by coordination to $\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}$ (p. 936). As the C atom is isoelectronic with $\{BH\}$ , so $\{C_{4}H_{4}\}$ is isoelectronic with the borane fragment $\{B_{4}H_{8}\}$ which is similarly stabilized by coordination to $\{\mathrm{Fe}(\mathrm{CO})_{3}\}$ or the isoelectronic $\{\mathrm{Co}(\eta^{5}-\mathrm{C}_{5}\mathrm{H}_{5})\}$ (see Panel on p. 174). Indeed it is a general feature of metallaborane chemistry that such clusters are often much more stable than are the parent boranes themselves.

As a result of the systematic application of coordination-chemistry principles, dozens of previously unsuspected structure types have been synthesized in which polyhedral boranes or their anions can be considered to act as ligands which donate electron density to metal centres, thereby forming novel metallaborane clusters. $^{(36,44,45,51-54)}$ Some 40 metals have been found to act as acceptors in this way (see also p. 178). The ideas have been particularly helpful in emphasizing the close interconnection between several previously separated branches of chemistry, notably boron hydride cluster chemistry, metallaborane and metallacarbaborane chemistry (pp. 189–95), organometallic chemistry and metal–metal cluster chemistry. All are now seen to be parts of a coherent whole.

It is also noteworthy that Alfred Stock, who is universally acclaimed as the discoverer of the boron hydrides (1912), $^{(10)}$ was also the first to propose the use of the term “ligand” (in a lecture in Berlin on 27 November 1916). $^{(55)}$ Both events essentially predate the formulation by G. N. Lewis of the electronic theory of valency (1916). It is therefore felicitous that, albeit some 20 years after Stock’s death in 1946, two such apparently disparate aspects of his work should be connected in the emerging concept of “boranes as ligands”.

![](Greenwood_元素化学_1-199_images/8899843fa4bb3b72321a43f0137024453a7e02cd5b0c3e9d7a4dc66190522663.jpg)  
procedure (pp. 162–3). Many of the product closo-boranes are not degraded even when heated to $600^{\circ}$ C. Salts of $B_{12}H_{12}^{2-}$ and $B_{10}H_{10}^{2-}$ are particularly stable and their reaction chemistry has been extensively studied. As expected from their charge, they are extremely stable towards nucleophiles but moderately susceptible to electrophilic attack. For $B_{10}H_{10}^{2-}$ the apex positions 1,10 are substituted preferentially to the equatorial positions; reference to structure (5) shows that there are 2 geometrical isomers for monosubstituted derivatives $B_{10}H_{9}X_{2}^{-}$ , 7 isomers for $B_{10}H_{8}X_{2}^{2-}$ and 16 for $B_{10}H_{7}X_{3}^{2-}$ . Many of these isomers exist, additionally, as enantiomeric pairs. Because of its higher symmetry $B_{12}H_{12}^{2-}$ has only 1 isomer for monosubstituted species $B_{12}H_{11}X^{2-}$ , 3 for $B_{12}H_{10}X_{2}^{2-}$ (sometimes referred to as ortho-, meta- and para-) and 5 for $B_{12}H_{9}X_{3}^{2-}$ . A particularly important derivative of $B_{12}H_{12}^{2-}$ is the thiol $[B_{12}H_{11}(SH)]^{2-}$ which has found use in the treatment of brain tumours by neutron capture therapy (see Panel on next page).

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 4} + 2 \mathrm{ZnR} _ {2} & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O}} [ \mathrm{B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2} \mathrm{Zn(Et} _ {2} \mathrm{O)} _ {2} ] \\ & \xrightarrow {\mathrm{H} _ {2} \mathrm{O/Me} _ {4} \mathrm{NCl}} [ \mathrm{NMe} _ {4} ] ^ {+} _ {2} [ \mathrm{Zn(B} _ {1 0} \mathrm{H} _ {1 2}) _ {2} ] ^ {2 -} \end{array}
$$

The structure of the highly reactive anion $[AlB_{10}H_{14}]^{-}$ is thought to be similar to nido- $B_{11}H_{14}^{-}$ with one facial B atom replaced by Al. The metal alkyls react somewhat differently to give extremely stable metalloborane anions which can be thought of as complexes of the bidentate ligand $B_{10}H_{12}^{2-}$ (structures 42, 43). $^{(44,51,52)}$ Many other complexes $[M(B_{10}H_{12})_{2}]^{2-}$ and $[L_{2}M(B_{10}H_{12})]$ are known with similar structures except that, where M = Ni, Pd, Pt, the coordination about the metal is essentially square-planar rather than pseudotetrahedral as for Zn, Cd and Hg. Such compounds were among the first examples to be recognized of the novel and extremely fruitful perception that “electron-deficient” boranes and their anions can, in fact, act as powerful stabilizing electron-donor ligands (see Panel on p. 177).

## 6.4.7 Chemistry of $\text{closo-B}_{n}\text{H}_{n}^{2-(1,56,57)}$

The structures of these anions have been indicated on p. 153. Preparative reactions are often mechanistically obscure but thermolysis under controlled conditions is the dominant

Oxidation of $closo-B_{10}H_{10}^{2-}$ with aqueous solutions of $Fe^{III}$ or $Ce^{IV}$ (or electrochemically) yields $conjuncto-B_{20}H_{18}^{2-}$ (44) which can be photoisomerized to $neo-B_{20}H_{18}^{2-}$ (45). If the oxidation is carried out at $0^{\circ}$ with $Ce^{IV}$ ,
